                                                                         2
                                                                                              0                        1

where 𝑘 is the external momentum, and 𝑚 0 and 𝑚 1 are the internal masses. 𝜇 is the t’Hooft normalisation scale.
    It is also noteworthy that this integral, unlike higher-point ones, is UV divergent and generates the pole term Δ 𝐵 ,

where 𝛾 𝐸 is Euler’s gamma constant. This function may also be written as an integral over the Feynman parameter 𝑥. That
yields the explicit analytical form [14],

B.2.2.2     The 𝐶0 Function
     The 𝐶0 function represents a scalar loop with three external lines and three internal propagators. It is a common feature in
the vertex corrections of electroweak physics. It is written in 𝑑 dimensions as,

                                                   (2𝜋𝜇) 4−𝑑
             𝐶0 (𝑘 1 , 𝑘 2 , 𝑚 0 , 𝑚 1 , 𝑚 2 ) =        2

    This integral is UV finite and may be rewritten as a double integral over the Feynman parameters 𝑥 and 𝑦 as,
                                                   ∫ 1        ∫ 1− 𝑥

B.2.2.3     The 𝐷 0 Function
     The 𝐷 0 function describes a scalar loop with four external lines and four internal propagators. It is often called the scalar
box integral. It is written as,
                                                                                                    ∫
                                                                                        (2𝜋𝜇) 4−𝑑                  1
                                     𝐷 0 (𝑘 1 , 𝑘 2 , 𝑘 3 , 𝑚 0 , 𝑚 1 , 𝑚 2 , 𝑚 3 ) =                   𝑑𝑑 𝑝                                      (B.25)
                                                                                           𝑖𝜋 2                𝐷0 𝐷1 𝐷2 𝐷3




                                                                                 103
with denominator factors,

                                                          𝐷 0 = 𝑝 2 − 𝑚 02 + 𝑖𝜀
                                                                       𝑖
                                                                              !2
                                                                      ∑︁                                                           (B.26)
                                                          𝐷𝑖 = 𝑝 +         𝑘 𝑎 − 𝑚 𝑖2 + 𝑖𝜀.
                                                                         𝑎=1

    Evaluating 𝐷 0 is highly non-trivial as it features numerous physical cuts and branch points. The resulting analytical
expression is expressed in terms of Spence functions (dilogarithms) [10].


B.3         IBP Reduction Example
    The method behind IBP reduction, as discussed in Subsection 2.5.1 may be made easier to visualise with an example.
Consider a one-loop bubble, akin to that studied via PaVe reduction in Section B.2. Take,
                                                                  ∫
                                                                            1
                                                 I (𝑎 0 , 𝑎 1 ) =   𝑑 𝑑 ℓ 𝑎0 𝑎1                                 (B.27)
                                                                         𝐷0 𝐷1

where,
                                                    𝐷 0 = ℓ 2 − 𝑚 02 ,      𝐷 1 = (ℓ + 𝑝) 2 − 𝑚 12 .                               (B.28)
We now choose 𝑣 𝜇 = ℓ 𝜇 , and build and expand the relation from Eq. (2.77) as,
                                        ∫                         !
                                              𝑑 𝜕           ℓ𝜇
                                   0=       𝑑 ℓ 𝜇         𝑎    𝑎
                                               𝜕ℓ       𝐷0 0 𝐷1 1
                                                             ∫                                      ∫                              (B.29)

       We can now use,

                                                                     ℓ 2 = 𝐷 0 + 𝑚 02                                              (B.30)

B.4         Three-Particle Phase-Space to Cut Propagators
       Let us now consider the massless three-particle phase-space Φ3 . It may be described as,
                                                                                        
                     𝑑Φ3 (𝑞; 𝑝 1 , 𝑝 2 , 𝑝 3 ) ∝ 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝 1 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝 2 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝 3 𝛿+ 𝑝 21 𝛿+ 𝑝 22 𝛿+ 𝑝 23 𝛿 (𝑑) (𝑞 − 𝑝 1 − 𝑝 2 − 𝑝 3 ).     (B.33)

We start by using momentum conservation to eliminate 𝑝 3 ,

                                                                  𝑝3 = 𝑞 − 𝑝1 − 𝑝2 .                                               (B.34)

This first step eliminates the rightmost 𝛿-function. Then, using reverse unitarity, each of the remaining 𝛿-functions may be
rewritten as cut propagators. Schematically, it gives,

                                                                                              1
                                              𝑑Φ3 ∼ 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝 1 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝 2                                                                (B.35)
                                                                      [ 𝑝 21 ] 𝑐 [ 𝑝 22 ] 𝑐 [(𝑞 − 𝑝 1 − 𝑝 2 ) 2 ] 𝑐

with                                                                             
                                                         1      1      1        1
                                                              ≡            −           .                                           (B.36)
                                                         𝑝 2 𝑐 2𝜋𝑖 𝑝 2 − 𝑖0 𝑝 2 + 𝑖0 +



                                                                           104
B.5       Laporta Algorithm Implementation Example
     Consider an arbitrary family defined as F [{𝐷 1 , 𝐷 2 , 𝐷 3 }; 𝑑Φ], with the denominators chosen as 𝐷 1 = 𝑠12 , 𝐷 2 = 𝑠13 and
𝐷 3 = 𝑠23 . We may take the integrals below, all members of this family, and organise them using their respective sector vectors
as follows,
                                                   ∫
                                                             𝑠13
                                              𝐼1 =     𝑑Φ         ⇒ 𝐼1 = 𝐼 (1, −1, 0),                                      (B.37)
                                                             𝑠12
                                                 ∫
                                                              2
                                           𝐼2 =     𝑑Φ               ⇒ 𝐼2 = 2𝐼 (1, 1, 1),                                   (B.38)
                                                        𝑠12 𝑠13 𝑠23
                                                    ∫
                                                               1
                                               𝐼3 =     𝑑Φ 3 ⇒ 𝐼3 = 𝐼 (3, 0, 0)                                             (B.39)
                                                              𝑠12

where 𝑑Φ is the common phase-space measure. Importantly, two integrals may share a sector if they contain the same
denominators, independently of those denominators’ powers. Also, only positive indices denote denominator presence, meaning
that integrals 𝐼1 and 𝐼3 in the example above are members of the same sector, 𝐼1 , 𝐼3 ∈ (1, 0, 0). In our example, only two sectors
appear: (1, 0, 0) and (1, 1, 1). These are our two target sectors. The three integrals are organised in terms of the 𝑡, 𝑟 and 𝑠 as
follows,
                                                                   𝑡 𝑟 𝑠
                                                       𝐼 (1, −1, 0)      1       0   1
                                                                                                                            (B.40)
                                                        𝐼 (1, 1, 1)      3       0   0
                                                        𝐼 (3, 0, 0)      1       2   0
then, by order of complexity, the algorithm orders the integrals as,

                                               𝐼 (1, 1, 1) ≻ 𝐼 (3, 0, 0) ≻ 𝐼 (1, −1, 0).                                    (B.41)


B.6       The Massive Extension
     The main body of this thesis focused primarily on the massless regime, since the vast majority of the present work’s chapters
focus on that regime. This appendix collects the supporting extension of the discussed methods into the massive regime.
     The fundamental change is the massive on-shell condition; the remainder of this section traces its consequences for the
antenna construction and integration. Since final-state particles now have mass, their squared momenta are not set to zero, they
are set to their squared mass.
                                                            Massive Regime
                                          𝑝 2𝑞 = 𝑝 2𝑞¯ = 0 −−−−−−−−−−−−
                                                                      → 𝑝 2𝑞 = 𝑝 2𝑞¯ = 𝑚 2𝑞 .                               (B.42)
where 𝑝 𝑖 represents the momentum and 𝑚 𝑖 represents the mass of particle 𝑖.
     The antenna subtraction formalism and the reduction architecture that was developed and described throughout Chapter 2
are retained, however. What changes is the intermediate steps and kinematics: the massive on-shell condition changes the
kinematics themselves and the unresolved limits, with soft radiation remaining singular but heavy-quark collinear behaviour
becoming quasi-collinear. Massive integration is also different, as the characteristic phase-space scale in the massive regime is
not equal to its massless counterpart. A new scale of 𝑚 2𝑞 /𝑞 2 is also introduced and leads to distinct cut-integral families and
master integrals [25].


B.6.1     Massive Kinematics and Infrared Structure
    In Subsection 2.2.1, the Mandelstam invariants were introduced for the massless regime. The general definition is,

                                             𝑠𝑖 𝑗 = ( 𝑝 𝑖 + 𝑝 𝑗 ) 2 = 𝑝 2𝑖 + 2𝑝 𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 + 𝑝 2𝑗
                                                                                                                            (B.43)
                                                                   = 𝑚 𝑖2 + 𝑚 2𝑗 + 2𝑝 𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 .
                                                    Í
Through momentum conservation, which states that 𝑞 = 𝑖 𝑝 𝑖 , we also have to reconsider the 𝑞 2 expression, as,
                                                   ∑︁          ∑︁
                                             𝑞2 =     𝑚 𝑖2 + 2     𝑝𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 .                                               (B.44)
                                                             𝑖            𝑖< 𝑗

     Soft radiation remains singular in the massive regime and therefore continues to generate explicit infrared poles after
integration. A non-zero mass regulates collinear limits involving a massive parton, whereas collinear limits between two
massless partons remain singular. If the two final-state collinear partons are massless, then the limit follows the massless


                                                                   105
collinear limit from the massless regime. If the two partons are massive, no strict collinear singularity exists. But if one of the
collinear partons is massive, consider that particle 𝑖 of mass 𝑚 𝑖 and energy 𝐸 𝑖 , and the other is massless, consider that particle
𝑗, such that 𝑚 𝑗 = 0, then,
                                                                              √︄
                                                                                      𝑚2
                                      𝑝 𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 = 𝐸 𝑖 𝐸 𝑗 (1 − 𝛽𝑖 cos 𝜃), 𝛽𝑖 = 1 − 2𝑖 < 1.                                    (B.45)
                                                                                      𝐸𝑖
As the angle between the partons 𝜃 tends towards zero, the mass prevents the invariant from reaching the collinear singular
surface,
                                            lim 𝑝 𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 = 𝐸 𝑖 𝐸 𝑗 (1 − 𝛽𝑖 ) ≠ 0.                                   (B.46)
                                                    𝜃→0
    This regulated collinear region still carries physically important information, particularly in the joint high-energy and
small-angle region, 𝐸 𝑖 >> 𝑚 𝑖 and 𝜃 << 1,                                  !
                                                             𝐸 𝑖 𝐸 𝑗 2 𝑚 𝑖2
                                                  𝑝𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗 ∼         𝜃 + 2                                             (B.47)
                                                                2       𝐸𝑖
and consequently since,
                                        2                        2
                                                                                              ! −1                 !
                                                                                       𝑚 𝑖2                 𝐸 𝑖2
                                     ∫ 𝜃max                   ∫ 𝜃max
                                                2   1                        2   2
                                              𝑑𝜃          ∼             𝑑𝜃       𝜃 +                 ∼ ln                     (B.48)
                                      0          𝑝𝑖 · 𝑝 𝑗       0                      𝐸 𝑖2                 𝑚 𝑖2
the massive-massless quasi-collinear region replaces the strict collinear pole by a mass logarithm. The quasi-collinear limit,
then, is the limit in which both the mass 𝑚 𝑖 and the opening angle 𝜃 become small together. It is through this limit that the
massive antennae recover the massless splitting behaviour, as 𝑚 𝑖 → 0 [25].
     Mass does not regulate UV divergences, as ℓ 2 >> 𝑚 𝑖2 , 𝑝 2𝑖 . It is noteworthy, however, that in a theory with massive quarks,
the quark mass and quark field must be renormalised as,
                                                                                      √︃
                                     𝑔𝑠,0 = 𝜇 𝜖 𝑍 𝑔 𝑔𝑠 , 𝑚 𝑞,0 = 𝑍 𝑚 𝑚 𝑞 , 𝜓𝑞,0 = 𝑍2𝑞 𝜓𝑞 .                                    (B.49)


B.6.2      Massive Phase-Space Integration and Reduction Methods
     For final-state particles of momenta 𝑝 𝑖 and masses 𝑚 𝑖 , the phase-space becomes,
                                                                       𝑛
                                                                             ! 𝑛
                                                                      ∑︁      Ö 𝑑 𝑑 𝑝𝑖                     
                                                             𝑑 (𝑑)                            +     2     2
                              𝑑Φ𝑛 (𝑞; 𝑝 1 , ..., 𝑝 𝑛 ) = (2𝜋) 𝛿    𝑞−     𝑝𝑖                𝛿     𝑝 𝑖 − 𝑚 𝑖 .                 (B.50)
                                                                      𝑖=1      𝑖=1
                                                                                   (2𝜋) 𝑑−1

At the level of the phase-space measure, the formal change from the massless case, as described in Subsubsection 2.5.1.2, is,

                                                        𝛿+ ( 𝑝 2𝑖 ) → 𝛿+ ( 𝑝 2𝑖 − 𝑚 𝑖2 )                                      (B.51)

which physically imposes that 𝑝 2𝑖 = 𝑚 𝑖2 and 𝑝 0𝑖 > 0.
    Phase-space factorisation still holds for the massive regime, but the momentum mapping must preserve the relevant
mass-shell conditions. So,
                                              𝑑Φ𝑚+𝑟 ({𝑝}; 𝑞) = 𝑑Φ𝑚 ({ ˜
                                                                     𝑝}; 𝑞)𝑑Φ𝑋 .                               (B.52)
For a final-final antenna with hard radiator 𝑖, 𝑘 and unresolved particles between them, this is schematically,

                                               𝑑Φ𝑚+𝑟 = 𝑑Φ𝑚 (..., 𝑝˜ 𝐼 , 𝑝˜ 𝐾 , ...; 𝑞)𝑑Φ𝑋𝑖 𝑗 𝑘                                (B.53)

while 𝑝˜2𝐼 = 𝑚 𝑖2 , 𝑝˜2𝐾 = 𝑚 2𝑘 and momentum conservation is satisfied,

                                                                     © ∑︁
                                                 𝑝˜ 𝐼 + 𝑝˜ 𝐾 = 𝑝 𝑖 + ­            𝑝 𝑗 ® + 𝑝𝑘 .
                                                                                      ª
                                                                                                                              (B.54)
                                                                     «unresolved 𝑗 ¬
     Antenna integration is computed in the same manner as in the massless regime,
                                               𝑙
                                                      !
                                   ∫          Ö                          𝜆+𝜅 𝜖  𝑚 2  
                               𝑙
                              X𝑛 ∼    𝑑Φ𝑋         𝑑 ℓ𝑟 𝑋𝑛𝑙 ({𝑝}, {ℓ}) ∼ 𝑞 2
                                                   𝑑
                                                                                 𝑓       ,𝜖 .                                 (B.55)
                                              𝑟=1
                                                                                    𝑞2

     PaVe and IBP reduction remain applicable in the massive regime, following the same principles introduced for the massless
case. For IBP reduction, however, the massive case is significantly harder analytically: the phase-space is non-trivial and has
mass-dependent thresholds; the master integrals are generally multi-scale, and can involve logarithms, polylogarithms and more
complicated transcendental functions; and lastly, boundary conditions are less direct, although common choices are the massless


                                                                     106
limit at 𝑚 → 0 and the threshold limit at 𝛽 → 0 [25].


B.7       Master Integral Substitution Arrays
     As discussed in Section B.5, AntCalc’s IBP reduction, handled internally by LiteRed2, organises each antenna family’s
master integrals as members of that family’s own basis, addressed through LiteRed2’s 𝑗 [basis, ...] notation rather than the named
masters used throughout Chapter 4. At the master combination stage for each antenna, the raw 𝑗 [...]-form result is substituted
through a rule list specific to that family’s basis, translating it into the named masters. The rule lists used are collected below,
one per antenna family.


B.7.1      The 𝐴22 Set
  In[1]:= A22MISubs = {

B.8       Local Validation of Additional Routes
     Much like the 𝐴30 massless unintegrated antenna was locally validated by computing its local soft and (single) collinear
limits, so have other antenna functions been validated in that way. In this section we present those validation methods and
results.


B.8.1      𝐴40
     The leading 𝐴40 (1𝑞 , 3𝑔 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ) massless antenna component has five relevant limits: two soft limits, and three collinear
limits, for 1𝑞 ||3𝑔 , 3𝑔 ||4𝑔 , and 2𝑞¯ ||4𝑔 . The two 𝑞𝑔 collinear limits, 1𝑞 ||3𝑔 and 2𝑞¯ ||4𝑔 , and the two soft-𝑔 limits are symmetric
under the reversal symmetry of the colour ordering, as 1 ↔ 2 and 3 ↔ 4.


B.8.1.1      Soft Limit and the Eikonal Factor
     In this limit we set 𝑝 𝑔 → 0. As mentioned, the limit is symmetric for both gluons. We will be focusing on the soft-3𝑔 here.
     The limit is thus computed as,

                                                                               (3) 0                      (3)      2𝑠14
                             lim 𝜆2 𝐴40                                     = S14 𝐴3 (1𝑞 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ),   S14  =           .       (B.56)
                            𝜆→0           𝑠13 ,𝑠23 ,𝑠34 →𝜆𝑠13 ,𝜆𝑠23 ,𝜆𝑠34                                         𝑠13 𝑠34

     This limit is computed using AntCalc as follows. Since the result here is larger than a simple factor like it was for 𝐴30 , the
result is presented as the difference of the limit and the product of the eikonal factor and the 𝐴30 antenna. Hence, the resulting
value we are looking for is zero [5].

   In[1]:= A40 = BuildAntenna[A,4,0,Component->Leading];

   soft3Rules = {s13 -> lambda s13, s23 -> lambda s23, s34 -> lambda s34,
   s134 -> s14, s234 -> s24, q2 -> s124};
   soft3Leading = SeriesCoefficient[A40 /. soft3Rules, {lambda, 0, -2}] // Simplify;

   A30142 = BuildAntenna[A,3,0] /. {s13 -> s14, s23 -> s24, q2 -> s124};
   soft3Expected = (2 s14/(s13 s34)) A30142 // Simplify;

   soft3Residual = FullSimplify[(soft3Leading - soft3Expected) /.



                                                                             108
  s124 -> s12 + s14 + s24]
  Out[1]:=
                                                                             0


B.8.1.2      Collinear 1𝑞 ||3𝑔 Limit
    For the 1𝑞 ||3𝑔 limit, the quark and gluon pair recombine into a parent quark as,

                                                           𝑝 1 = 𝑧 𝑝 13 ,   𝑝 3 = (1 − 𝑧) 𝑝 13                                       (B.57)

where 𝑧 is the momentum fraction carried by the original quark. This step makes 𝑠13 vanish as, 𝑠13 → 0.
    We now introduce the Mandelstam notation 𝑠 (13) 𝑗 to represent the invariant computed from the momenta of the parent
quark and some other 𝑗 particle. The remaining invariants become,

                                                      𝑠12 → 𝑧𝑠 (13)2 ,      𝑠23 → (1 − 𝑧)𝑠 (13)2 ,
                                                      𝑠14 → 𝑧𝑠 (13)4 ,      𝑠34 → (1 − 𝑧)𝑠 (13)4 ,                                   (B.58)
                                                                       𝑠24 → 𝑠24 .

We also define the reduced configuration of the three-parton tree-level antenna as,
                                                                         
                                                      𝐴30 (13)𝑞 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ .                                                         (B.59)

    At leading power, the four-parton antenna factorises as,

                                                  1                                                          1 + 𝑧2
           lim 𝑠13 𝐴40 (1𝑞 , 3𝑔 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ) =       𝑃𝑞𝑔→𝑞 (𝑧, 𝜖) 𝐴30 (13)𝑞 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ,      𝑃𝑞𝑔→𝑞 (𝑧, 𝜖) =          − 𝜖 (1 − 𝑧).   (B.60)
          𝑠13 →0                                 𝑠13                                                           1−𝑧

Here 𝑃𝑞𝑔→𝑞 (𝑧, 𝜖) is the O (𝜖)-extended version of the splitting function used for 𝐴30 ’s collinear limit in Section 4.1.2.2, needed
since 𝐴40 is an NNLO antenna and the 𝜖-dependent term no longer drops out of the check [5].

  In[1]:= A40 = BuildAntenna[A,4,0,Component->Leading];

  col13Rules = {s13 -> lambda, s12 -> z sP2, s23 -> (1-z) sP2, s14 -> z sP4,
  s34 -> (1-z) sP4, s134 -> sP4, s234 -> (1-z) sP2 + s24 + (1-z) sP4,
  q2 -> sP2 + sP4 + s24, Epsilon -> 0};
  col13Leading = SeriesCoefficient[Together[A40 /. col13Rules], {lambda, 0, -1}] //
  FullSimplify;

  A30P42 = BuildAntenna[A,3,0] /. {s12 -> sP2, s13 -> sP4, s23 -> s24,
  q2 -> sP2 + sP4 + s24, Epsilon -> 0};

  Pqg = (1 + zˆ2)/(1 - z);

  FullSimplify[col13Leading - Pqg A30P42]
  Out[1]:=
                                                                             0


B.8.1.3      Collinear 3𝑔 ||4𝑔 Limit
    For the 3𝑔 ||4𝑔 limit, the gluon-gluon pair recombine into a parent gluon as,

                                                          𝑝 3 = 𝑧 𝑝 34 ,    𝑝 4 = (1 − 𝑧) 𝑝 34 .                                     (B.61)

This step makes 𝑠34 vanish.
    The remaining invariants become,
                                                      𝑠13 → 𝑧𝑠1(34) ,       𝑠14 → (1 − 𝑧)𝑠1(34) ,
                                                      𝑠23 → 𝑧𝑠 (34)2 ,      𝑠24 → (1 − 𝑧)𝑠 (34)2 ,                                   (B.62)
                                                                       𝑠12 → 𝑠12 .



                                                                            109
The reduced configuration of the three-parton tree-level antenna also becomes,
                                                                         
                                                      𝐴30 1𝑞 , (34)𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ .                                              (B.63)

    The parent parton here is now a gluon, not a quark. Hence, the local factorisation formula involves the spin-correlated
𝑔 → 𝑔𝑔 splitting tensor:
                                                         3𝑔 | |4𝑔  1 ˆ 𝜇𝜈                                  
                              𝐴40 (1𝑞 , 3𝑔 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ) −−−−−→        𝑃𝑔𝑔→𝐺 (𝑧, 𝑘 ⊥ ) 𝐴30 𝜇𝜈 1𝑞 , (34)𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ .        (B.64)
                                                                  𝑠34
          
Here, 𝐴30 𝜇𝜈 is the reduced three-parton antenna with the parent-gluon polarisation indices left uncontracted. Equivalently, in
terms of scalar antenna functions, the unaveraged collinear limit contains an angular contribution,
                                                      3𝑔 | |4𝑔    1                               
                              𝐴40 (1𝑞 , 3𝑔 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ) −−−−−→       𝑃𝑔𝑔→𝐺 (𝑧) 𝐴30 1𝑞 , (34)𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ + ang.,               (B.65)
                                                                 𝑠34
where ang. denotes a spin-correlation contribution. This term vanishes after integration over the unresolved azimuthal angle.
Consequently,                                                                                     
                               lim 𝑠34 𝐴40 (1𝑞 , 3𝑔 , 4𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ) 𝜙 = 𝑃𝑔𝑔→𝐺 (𝑧) 𝐴30 1𝑞 , (34)𝑔 , 2𝑞¯ ,                 (B.66)
                                𝑠34 →0

where                                                                                   
                                                                      𝑧    1−𝑧
                                            𝑃𝑔𝑔→𝐺 (𝑧) = 2                +     + 𝑧(1 − 𝑧) .                               (B.67)
                                                                     1−𝑧    𝑧
The direct invariant-space limit produces a non-vanishing residual after subtracting the spin-averaged contribution, consistently
with the angular term in the local gluon–gluon collinear factorisation [5].

  In[1]:= A40 = BuildAntenna[A,4,0,Component->Leading];

  col34Rules = {s34 -> lambda, s13 -> z s1P, s14 -> (1-z) s1P, s23 -> z sP2,
  s24 -> (1-z) sP2, s134 -> s1P + lambda, s234 -> sP2 + lambda,
  q2 -> s12 + s1P + sP2 + lambda, Epsilon -> 0};
  col34Leading = SeriesCoefficient[Together[A40 /. col34Rules], {lambda, 0, -1}] //
  FullSimplify;

  A301P2 = BuildAntenna[A,3,0] /. {s13 -> s1P, s23 -> sP2,
  q2 -> s12 + s1P + sP2, Epsilon -> 0};

  Pgg = 2 (z/(1-z) + (1-z)/z + z (1-z));
  spinCorrelation34 = 4 s12 (z-1) z/(s1P sP2);

  FullSimplify[col34Leading - (Pgg A301P2 + spinCorrelation34)]
  Out[1]:=
                                          0
